# Remaining Documentation Cross-Check — Getting Started, Guides, Integrations, Developer Guide, User-Guide Top Level, Reference Misc

This shard inventories every documented feature, procedure, integration and developer-facing API that lives in
`website/docs/getting-started/**`, `website/docs/guides/**`, `website/docs/integrations/**`,
`website/docs/developer-guide/**`, the top-level `website/docs/user-guide/*.md` pages
(cli, tui, desktop, docker, windows-native, windows-wsl-quickstart, profiles, profile-distributions, sessions,
bot-mode, checkpoints-and-rollback, git-worktrees, import-from-other-agents, managed-scope,
multi-connection-desktop, multi-profile-gateways, which-file-does-what, configuration, configuring-models),
`website/docs/reference/{faq.md,cli-symbols.md}`, plus `website/docs/index.mdx` and `website/docs/user-stories.mdx`.
Entries quote the docs' own terminology verbatim and are verified against the shipped source where possible.
Deliberately left to sibling shards: the per-feature pages under `user-guide/features/**`, `user-guide/messaging/**`,
`user-guide/skills/**`, `user-guide/secrets/**`, `user-guide/egress/**`, `user-guide/security.md` (docs-features shard);
the exhaustive CLI flag/command surface (cli-a…cli-f), the config-key catalogue (config-a/config-b),
env vars (env-vars), tools/toolsets (tools), dashboard/desktop/TUI UI strings (web-*, desktop-*, tui),
and the reference catalogs (cli-commands, slash-commands, tools-reference, toolsets-reference, model-catalog,
skills-catalog, optional-skills-catalog, automation-blueprints-catalog, mcp-config-reference, profile-commands,
environment-variables).

## Getting Started

### Hermes Agent Quickstart page  `id: docs-rest.quickstart`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Docs site → **Getting Started** → **"Hermes Agent Quickstart"** (`website/docs/getting-started/quickstart.md`, `sidebar_position: 1`, slug `/getting-started/quickstart`). Sidebar category label **"Getting Started"** with generated-index description **"Get up and running with Hermes Agent in minutes."** (`website/docs/getting-started/_category_.json:2-7`).
- **What it does:** The canonical zero-to-working-chat path: install → choose provider → verify a chat → verify session resume → try key features → add the next layer. It is written so that a reader never adds a feature before a plain chat works.
- **How it works:** Static Docusaurus MDX page. Sections in order: "Prefer to watch?" (embedded YouTube `https://www.youtube-nocookie.com/embed/R3YOGfTBcQg`, title **"Hermes Agent Masterclass: Installation, Setup, Basic Commands"**, plus the playlist link **"Hermes Agent Tutorials & Use Cases"** → `https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLmpUb_PWAkDxewld5ZYyKifuHxgIbiq2d`, credited to **"Onchain AI Garage"**), "Who this is for", "The fastest path", "1. Install Hermes Agent", "2. Choose a Provider", "3. Run Your First Chat", "4. Verify Sessions Work", "5. Try Key Features", "6. Add the Next Layer", "Common Failure Modes", "Recovery Toolkit", "Quick Reference", "Next Steps".
- **Inputs / options:** "Who this is for" bullets, verbatim: "Brand new and want the shortest path to a working setup"; "Switching providers and don't want to lose time to config mistakes"; "Setting up Hermes for a team, bot, or always-on workflow"; "Tired of \"it installed, but it still does nothing\"". Rule of thumb, verbatim: "if Hermes cannot complete a normal chat, do not add more features yet. Get one clean conversation working first, then layer on gateway, cron, skills, voice, or routing."
- **Outputs / side effects:** None (documentation). Links out to `../user-guide/cli.md`, `../user-guide/configuration.md`, `../user-guide/messaging/index.md`, `../user-guide/features/tools.md`, `../integrations/providers.md`, `../user-guide/features/skills.md`, `../guides/tips.md`, `/reference/faq#exporting-hermes-to-another-machine`.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** n/a
- **Rebuild notes:** A quickstart must be ordered by *verification gates*, not by feature list: install → provider → one successful chat → session resume → only then extras. A better version would embed a copy-pasteable `hermes doctor --json` self-check between each gate so the reader can prove the gate passed.

### Quickstart "The fastest path" goal router  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-fastest-path`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Quickstart → section **"The fastest path"** ("Pick the row that matches your goal").
- **What it does:** A 5-row decision table mapping a user goal to the first command and the follow-up step.
- **How it works:** Markdown table, columns **"Goal" | "Do this first" | "Then do this"**.
- **Inputs / options:** All 5 rows verbatim — (1) "I just want Hermes working on my machine" → `hermes setup` → "Run a real chat and verify it responds"; (2) "I already know my provider" → `hermes model` → "Save the config, then start chatting"; (3) "I want a bot or always-on setup" → `hermes gateway setup` after CLI works → "Connect Telegram, Discord, Slack, or another platform"; (4) "I want a local or self-hosted model" → `hermes model` → custom endpoint → "Verify the endpoint, model name, and context length"; (5) "I want multi-provider fallback" → `hermes model` first → "Add routing and fallback only after the base chat works".
- **Outputs / side effects:** n/a
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** n/a
- **Rebuild notes:** Goal-indexed routing table; 3 columns, one row per intent. Better: make each row a clickable "run this" button in an interactive docs shell.

### `hermes setup` three setup modes (Quick Setup / Full Setup / Blank Slate)  `id: docs-rest.setup-modes`
- **Surface:** CLI / Docs
- **Where:** Quickstart → "2. Choose a Provider" → admonition **":::info Setup modes"**; also `hermes setup --portal` in the tip **":::tip Easiest path: Nous Portal"**.
- **What it does:** Documents that on a fresh install `hermes setup` offers three modes, and exactly what each mode turns on or off.
- **How it works:** Verbatim from the doc: **"Quick Setup (Nous Portal)"** — "free OAuth login, no API keys; sets up a model plus the Tool Gateway tools. The recommended fast path." **"Full Setup"** — "walk through every provider, tool, and option yourself (bring your own keys)." **"Blank Slate"** — "everything starts **off** except the bare minimum needed to run an agent: **provider & model, the File Operations toolset, and the Terminal toolset**. No web, browser, code execution, vision, memory, delegation, cron, skills, plugins, or MCP servers — and compression, checkpoints, smart routing, and memory capture are all disabled." After the minimal baseline, the user picks one of two paths: **"start with everything disabled"** (finish now with the minimal agent) or **"walk through all configurations"** (opt in to tools, skills, plugins, MCP, and messaging). Blank Slate writes an explicit `platform_toolsets.cli` list plus `agent.disabled_toolsets`, "so nothing you didn't choose ever loads — not even after `hermes update`".
- **Inputs / options:** `hermes setup` (interactive, no args); `hermes setup --portal` ("logs you in, sets Nous as your provider, and turns on the Tool Gateway in one command"); recovery/expansion commands named by the doc: `hermes tools` (re-enable toolsets), `hermes skills opt-in --sync` (seed skills), `hermes setup agent` (tune settings).
- **Outputs / side effects:** Writes `platform_toolsets.cli` and `agent.disabled_toolsets` into `~/.hermes/config.yaml` for Blank Slate; Quick Setup writes provider/model + Tool Gateway settings and an OAuth credential.
- **Config / env:** `platform_toolsets.cli`, `agent.disabled_toolsets`; Tool Gateway keys documented on `../user-guide/features/tool-gateway.md`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** Blank Slate's explicit toolset list survives `hermes update` (update-time toolset additions do not silently re-enable).
- **Rebuild notes:** Implement three named setup profiles that differ only in the *initial value* of the enablement matrix, and always persist an explicit allowlist (never "absence = default on") so upgrades cannot widen the surface. Better: emit a diffable `setup-receipt.json` naming every key the chosen mode wrote.

### Quickstart provider catalog (43 providers)  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-provider-table`
- **Surface:** Docs / Provider
- **Where:** Quickstart → "2. Choose a Provider" → the **"Provider | What it is | How to set up"** table ("Good defaults:").
- **What it does:** Names every provider a first-time user can pick from `hermes model`, with a one-line description and the exact env var or OAuth path.
- **How it works:** Static table; the authoritative catalog is `../integrations/providers.md`. All 43 rows verbatim (Provider — What it is — How to set up):
  1. **Nous Portal** — "Subscription-based, zero-config" — "OAuth login via `hermes model`".
  2. **OpenAI Codex** — "ChatGPT or Codex subscription, uses Codex models" — "Device code auth via `hermes model` → **ChatGPT or Codex Subscription**".
  3. **Anthropic** — "Claude models directly — Max plan + extra usage credits (OAuth), or API key for pay-per-token" — "`hermes model` → OAuth login (requires Max + extra credits), or an Anthropic API key".
  4. **OpenRouter** — "Multi-provider routing across many models" — "Enter your API key".
  5. **Fireworks AI** — "Direct OpenAI-compatible model API" — "Set `FIREWORKS_API_KEY`".
  6. **Z.AI** — "GLM / Zhipu-hosted models" — "Set `GLM_API_KEY` / `ZAI_API_KEY` (also accepts `Z_AI_API_KEY`)".
  7. **Kimi / Moonshot** — "Moonshot-hosted coding and chat models" — "Set `KIMI_API_KEY` (or the Kimi-Coding-specific `KIMI_CODING_API_KEY`)".
  8. **Kimi / Moonshot China** — "China-region Moonshot endpoint" — "Set `KIMI_CN_API_KEY`".
  9. **Arcee AI** — "Trinity models" — "Set `ARCEEAI_API_KEY`".
  10. **GMI Cloud** — "Multi-model direct API" — "Set `GMI_API_KEY`".
  11. **Actual Computer** — "Your own hardware as a private inference cluster — hosted relay or local daemon" — "Set `ACTUAL_API_KEY` (relay) or `ACTUAL_BASE_URL=http://127.0.0.1:8080` (local, no key)".
  12. **MiniMax (OAuth)** — "MiniMax frontier model via browser OAuth — no API key needed (model name in `hermes_cli/models.py` may change between releases)" — "`hermes model` → MiniMax (OAuth)".
  13. **MiniMax** — "International MiniMax endpoint" — "Set `MINIMAX_API_KEY`".
  14. **MiniMax China** — "China-region MiniMax endpoint" — "Set `MINIMAX_CN_API_KEY`".
  15. **Alibaba Cloud** — "Qwen models via DashScope" — "Set `DASHSCOPE_API_KEY` (Qwen Coding Plan also accepts `ALIBABA_CODING_PLAN_API_KEY`)".
  16. **Hugging Face** — "20+ open models via unified router (Qwen, DeepSeek, Kimi, etc.)" — "Set `HF_TOKEN`".
  17. **AWS Bedrock** — "Claude, Nova, Llama, DeepSeek via native Converse API" — "IAM role or `aws configure` ([guide](../guides/aws-bedrock.md))".
  18. **Azure Foundry** — "Azure AI Foundry-hosted models" — "Set `AZURE_FOUNDRY_API_KEY` + `AZURE_FOUNDRY_BASE_URL`".
  19. **Google AI Studio** — "Gemini models via direct API" — "Set `GOOGLE_API_KEY` / `GEMINI_API_KEY`".
  20. **xAI** — "Grok models via direct API" — "Set `XAI_API_KEY`".
  21. **xAI Grok OAuth** — "SuperGrok / Premium+ subscription, no API key needed" — "`hermes model` → xAI Grok OAuth".
  22. **NovitaAI** — "Multi-model API gateway" — "Set `NOVITA_API_KEY`".
  23. **Ramp Router** — "Responses-native LLM gateway routing across OpenAI/Anthropic/xAI/..." — "Set `RAMP_ROUTER_API_KEY`".
  24. **Nebius Token Factory** — "Open models on Nebius AI cloud" — "Set `NEBIUS_API_KEY`".
  25. **StepFun** — "Step Plan models" — "Set `STEPFUN_API_KEY`".
  26. **Xiaomi MiMo** — "Xiaomi-hosted models" — "Set `XIAOMI_API_KEY`".
  27. **Tencent TokenHub** — "Tencent-hosted models" — "Set `TOKENHUB_API_KEY`".
  28. **Tencent TokenPlan** — "Tencent Hy models via Anthropic-style endpoint" — "Set `TOKENPLAN_API_KEY`".
  29. **Ollama Cloud** — "Managed Ollama-hosted models" — "Set `OLLAMA_API_KEY`".
  30. **LM Studio** — "Local desktop app exposing an OpenAI-compatible API" — "Set `LM_API_KEY` (and `LM_BASE_URL` if non-default)".
  31. **Qwen OAuth** — "Qwen Portal browser OAuth — no API key needed" — "`hermes model` → Qwen OAuth".
  32. **Kilo Code** — "KiloCode-hosted models" — "Set `KILOCODE_API_KEY`".
  33. **OpenCode Zen** — "Pay-as-you-go access to curated models" — "Set `OPENCODE_ZEN_API_KEY`".

- **Config / env:** All env vars listed above.
- **Edge cases / guards:** Doc notes "You can switch providers at any time with `hermes model` — no lock-in."
- **Rebuild notes:** Generate the table from the provider registry rather than hand-maintaining it, so a new provider cannot ship undocumented. Handoff: full provider semantics live in `docs-rest.integrations-providers` and the `providers` shard.

### Minimum context requirement: 64K tokens  `id: docs-rest.min-context-64k`
- **Surface:** Docs / Core
- **Where:** Quickstart → "2. Choose a Provider" → admonition **":::caution Minimum context: 64K tokens"**.
- **What it does:** States that Hermes requires a model with at least **64,000 tokens** of context and that smaller models are rejected at startup.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "Models with smaller windows cannot maintain enough working memory for multi-step tool-calling workflows and will be rejected at startup. Most hosted models (Claude, GPT, Gemini, Qwen, DeepSeek) meet this easily. If you're running a local model, set its context size to at least 64K (e.g. `--ctx-size 65536` for llama.cpp or `-c 65536` for Ollama)."
- **Inputs / options:** llama.cpp `--ctx-size 65536`; Ollama `-c 65536`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Startup rejection when the model's declared context is below the floor.
- **Config / env:** Governed by the model catalog's context length metadata (see `docs-rest.configuring-models`).
- **Edge cases / guards:** Local/self-hosted models frequently default well below 64K and must be re-launched with a larger window.
- **Rebuild notes:** Enforce a hard context floor at model-selection time with a named constant, and surface the measured window (not the advertised one) in the rejection message. Better: degrade to a "small-context mode" with aggressive compression instead of refusing outright.

### Settings storage split: `.env` vs `config.yaml`  `id: docs-rest.settings-storage-split`
- **Surface:** Docs / Config
- **Where:** Quickstart → "2. Choose a Provider" → subsection **"### How settings are stored"**.
- **What it does:** Explains that Hermes separates secrets from non-secret config and that `hermes config set` routes each value to the right file automatically.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "**Secrets and tokens** → `~/.hermes/.env`"; "**Non-secret settings** → `~/.hermes/config.yaml`". "The right value goes to the right file automatically."
- **Inputs / options:** Example commands, verbatim: `hermes config set model anthropic/claude-opus-4.6`; `hermes config set terminal.backend docker`; `hermes config set OPENROUTER_API_KEY sk-or-...`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Writes to `~/.hermes/.env` (secret-shaped keys, i.e. ALL_CAPS env names) or `~/.hermes/config.yaml` (dotted keys).
- **Config / env:** `model`, `terminal.backend`, `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` shown as examples.
- **Edge cases / guards:** Under NixOS/Home-Manager managed mode `hermes config set` is blocked (see `docs-rest.nix-managed-mode`).
- **Rebuild notes:** Route by key shape (uppercase+underscore ⇒ secret file; dotted lowercase ⇒ YAML), and never write a secret into the YAML even if the user asks. Better: back the secret file with an OS keychain and keep `.env` as a fallback.

### First chat verification (`hermes` vs `hermes --tui`)  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-first-chat`
- **Surface:** Docs / CLI / TUI
- **Where:** Quickstart → "3. Run Your First Chat".
- **What it does:** Tells the user to start a chat and gives three verifiable prompts plus four success criteria.
- **How it works:** Commands, verbatim: `hermes` ("classic CLI") and `hermes --tui` ("modern TUI (recommended)"). Tip **":::tip Pick your interface"** — "Hermes ships with two terminal interfaces: the classic `prompt_toolkit` CLI and a newer [TUI](../user-guide/tui.md) with modal overlays, mouse selection, and non-blocking input. Both share the same sessions, slash commands, and config — try each with `hermes` vs `hermes --tui`."
- **Inputs / options:** The three suggested prompts, verbatim: "Summarize this repo in 5 bullets and tell me what the main entrypoint is."; "Check my current directory and tell me what looks like the main project file."; "Help me set up a clean GitHub PR workflow for this codebase."
- **Outputs / side effects:** "You'll see a welcome banner with your model, available tools, and skills."
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** "**What success looks like:**" — "The banner shows your chosen model/provider"; "Hermes replies without error"; "It can use a tool if needed (terminal, file read, web search)"; "The conversation continues normally for more than one turn".
- **Rebuild notes:** Ship *verifiable* first prompts (ones whose correctness the user can eyeball), plus an explicit success checklist. Better: a `hermes selftest` that runs these three prompts and grades the answers.

### Session-resume verification (`hermes --continue` / `-c`)  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-verify-sessions`
- **Surface:** Docs / CLI
- **Where:** Quickstart → "4. Verify Sessions Work".
- **What it does:** Instructs the user to confirm session persistence before adding features.
- **How it works:** `hermes --continue` ("Resume the most recent session"), `hermes -c` ("Short form"). Failure hint, verbatim: "If it doesn't, check whether you're in the same profile and whether the session actually saved."
- **Inputs / options:** `--continue`, `-c`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Reopens the most recent session from the session store.
- **Config / env:** Profile selection affects which session store is read.
- **Edge cases / guards:** Profile mismatch and unsaved sessions are the two named causes of failure.
- **Rebuild notes:** Persistence must be verifiable in one command before any further feature onboarding. Better: `--continue` should say *which* session and profile it resumed and when it was last written.

### Quickstart slash-command sampler  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-slash-sampler`
- **Surface:** Docs / CLI
- **Where:** Quickstart → "5. Try Key Features" → **"### Slash commands"**.
- **What it does:** Shows that typing `/` opens an autocomplete dropdown of all commands, and lists five starter commands.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "Type `/` to see an autocomplete dropdown of all commands".
- **Inputs / options:** All five rows verbatim — `/help` "Show all available commands"; `/tools` "List available tools"; `/model` "Switch models interactively"; `/personality pirate` "Try a fun personality"; `/save` "Save the conversation".
- **Outputs / side effects:** n/a
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** n/a
- **Rebuild notes:** A `/` trigger that opens a fuzzy command palette is the discoverability primitive; the docs only need to name five. Handoff: complete slash surface is the `gw-slash` shard.

### Multi-line input key bindings  `id: docs-rest.multiline-input`
- **Surface:** Docs / CLI / TUI
- **Where:** Quickstart → "5. Try Key Features" → **"### Multi-line input"**.
- **What it does:** Documents the three key chords that insert a newline instead of submitting, and which terminals support each.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "Press `Alt+Enter`, `Ctrl+J`, or `Shift+Enter` to add a new line. `Shift+Enter` requires a terminal that sends it as a distinct sequence (Kitty / foot / WezTerm / Ghostty by default; iTerm2 / Alacritty / VS Code terminal once the Kitty keyboard protocol is enabled). `Alt+Enter` and `Ctrl+J` work in every terminal."
- **Inputs / options:** `Alt+Enter`, `Ctrl+J`, `Shift+Enter`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Inserts a newline into the prompt buffer.
- **Config / env:** Requires the Kitty keyboard protocol for `Shift+Enter` on iTerm2 / Alacritty / VS Code terminal.
- **Edge cases / guards:** Terminals that do not emit a distinct `Shift+Enter` sequence fall back to submit-on-Enter.
- **Rebuild notes:** Always provide two protocol-independent chords (`Alt+Enter`, `Ctrl+J`) alongside the nicer `Shift+Enter`, and detect Kitty-protocol support at startup. Better: probe the terminal once and print which chord will work.

### Interrupting the agent mid-task  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-interrupt`
- **Surface:** Docs / CLI
- **Where:** Quickstart → "5. Try Key Features" → **"### Interrupt the agent"**.
- **What it does:** Documents that typing a new message and pressing Enter interrupts the running task and switches to the new instruction; `Ctrl+C` also works.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "If the agent is taking too long, type a new message and press Enter — it interrupts the current task and switches to your new instructions. `Ctrl+C` also works."
- **Inputs / options:** New message + Enter; `Ctrl+C`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Cancels the in-flight turn; the new user message becomes the next instruction.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** n/a
- **Rebuild notes:** Keep the input box live while the agent streams, and treat a submitted message as an implicit cancel-then-enqueue. Better: offer "queue after current step" vs "cancel now" as two distinct submits.

### Quickstart "Add the Next Layer" checklist  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-next-layer`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Quickstart → "6. Add the Next Layer" ("Only after the base chat works. Pick what you need:").
- **What it does:** Enumerates the seven optional layers in the recommended order of adoption with the exact command for each.
- **How it works:** Sub-sections and their commands, verbatim:
  - **"### Bot or shared assistant"** — `hermes gateway setup` ("Interactive platform configuration"); connect **Telegram**, **Discord**, **Slack**, **WhatsApp**, **Signal**, **Email**, **Home Assistant**, **Microsoft Teams**.
  - **"### Automation and tools"** — `hermes tools` ("tune tool access per platform"); `hermes skills` ("browse and install reusable workflows"); "Cron — only after your bot or CLI setup is stable".
  - **"### Sandboxed terminal"** — `hermes config set terminal.backend docker` ("Docker isolation"); `hermes config set terminal.backend ssh` ("Remote server"); egress proxy: `hermes egress setup && hermes egress start`; "Modal, SSH, Daytona, and Singularity are not wired yet" (for credential injection).
  - **"### Voice mode"** — `cd ~/.hermes/hermes-agent` then `uv pip install --python ./venv/bin/python -e ".[voice]"` ("Includes faster-whisper for free local speech-to-text"); then `/voice on` in the CLI, `Ctrl+B` to record. Install directory on Windows: `%LOCALAPPDATA%\hermes\hermes-agent`.
  - **"### Skills"** — `hermes skills browse` ("list everything available"); `hermes skills search kubernetes` ("find skills by keyword"); `hermes skills install openai/skills/k8s` ("install one (runs a security scan first)"); usage `/k8s deploy the staging manifest` and bare `/k8s`.
  - **"### MCP servers"** — YAML block added to `~/.hermes/config.yaml` under `mcp_servers:` with `github: {command: npx, args: ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-github"], env: {GITHUB_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN: "ghp_xxx"}}`.
  - **"### Editor integration (ACP)"** — `hermes acp`; "ACP support ships with the standard `[all]` extras"; fallback install `cd ~/.hermes/hermes-agent && uv pip install -e ".[acp]"`.
- **Inputs / options:** As listed above.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Each command mutates config/state in its own subsystem.
- **Config / env:** `terminal.backend` (`docker` | `ssh`); `mcp_servers.<name>.{command,args,env}`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** Skills install "runs a security scan first"; the install slug is a `source/path` pair — "`openai/skills/k8s` means the `k8s` skill from OpenAI's catalog"; "every installed skill becomes a slash command automatically" and works "in the CLI and in any connected messaging platform".
- **Rebuild notes:** Order optional subsystems by prerequisite depth and gate each behind a working base. Better: a single `hermes layers` command that shows which layers are on, off, and blocked.

### Quickstart "Common Failure Modes" table  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-failure-modes`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Quickstart → **"## Common Failure Modes"** ("These are the problems that waste the most time:").
- **What it does:** Six-row symptom → likely cause → fix triage table.
- **How it works:** All six rows verbatim (Symptom | Likely cause | Fix):
  1. "Hermes opens but gives empty or broken replies" | "Provider auth or model selection is wrong" | "Run `hermes model` again and confirm provider, model, and auth".
  2. "Custom endpoint \"works\" but returns garbage" | "Wrong base URL, model name, or not actually OpenAI-compatible" | "Verify the endpoint in a separate client first".
  3. "Gateway starts but nobody can message it" | "Bot token, allowlist, or platform setup is incomplete" | "Re-run `hermes gateway setup` and check `hermes gateway status`".
  4. "`hermes --continue` can't find old session" | "Switched profiles or session never saved" | "Check `hermes sessions list` and confirm you're in the right profile".
  5. "Model unavailable or odd fallback behavior" | "Provider routing or fallback settings are too aggressive" | "Keep routing off until the base provider is stable".
  6. "`hermes doctor` flags config problems" | "Config values are missing or stale" | "Fix the config, retest a plain chat before adding features".
- **Outputs / side effects:** n/a
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** n/a
- **Rebuild notes:** Triage tables must key on the *observable symptom*, not the subsystem. Better: have `hermes doctor` emit these exact symptom IDs so the docs and the tool share a vocabulary.

### Quickstart "Recovery Toolkit" ordered sequence  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-recovery-toolkit`
- **Surface:** Docs / CLI
- **Where:** Quickstart → **"## Recovery Toolkit"** ("When something feels off, use this order:").
- **What it does:** Prescribes a fixed six-step diagnostic order.
- **How it works:** Ordered list, verbatim: 1. `hermes doctor`; 2. `hermes model`; 3. `hermes setup`; 4. `hermes sessions list`; 5. `hermes --continue`; 6. `hermes gateway status`. Closing line: "That sequence gets you from \"broken vibes\" back to a known state fast."
- **Inputs / options:** Six commands as listed.
- **Outputs / side effects:** `hermes setup` rewrites config; the rest are read-only or session-restoring.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** n/a
- **Rebuild notes:** Order recovery steps cheapest-and-least-destructive first. Better: `hermes recover` that runs the read-only steps automatically and only prompts before the mutating one.

### Quickstart "Quick Reference" command table  `id: docs-rest.quickstart-quick-reference`
- **Surface:** Docs / CLI
- **Where:** Quickstart → **"## Quick Reference"**.
- **What it does:** Eight-row cheat sheet of the commands a new user needs.
- **How it works:** All eight rows verbatim (Command | Description): `hermes` "Start chatting"; `hermes model` "Choose your LLM provider and model"; `hermes tools` "Configure which tools are enabled per platform"; `hermes setup` "Full setup wizard (configures everything at once)"; `hermes doctor` "Diagnose issues"; `hermes update` "Update to latest version"; `hermes gateway` "Start the messaging gateway"; `hermes --continue` "Resume last session".
- **Inputs / options:** n/a
- **Outputs / side effects:** n/a
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** n/a
- **Rebuild notes:** Keep the cheat sheet to eight commands maximum; anything more belongs in the full CLI reference.

### Installation page (installers for every platform)  `id: docs-rest.installation`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Docs → Getting Started → **"Installation"** (`website/docs/getting-started/installation.md`, `sidebar_position: 2`).
- **What it does:** The canonical install instructions: desktop installer, one-line curl/PowerShell installers, what the installer does, install layout, post-install commands.
- **How it works:** Install commands verbatim —
  - Desktop installer (macOS/Windows, "recommended"): "download the Hermes Desktop installer" from `https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/`.
  - Linux / macOS / WSL2 / Android (Termux): `curl -fsSL https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/install.sh | bash`.
  - Windows (native), in PowerShell: `iex (irm https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/install.ps1)`.
  - Post-CLI desktop launch: `hermes desktop`.
  - After install: `source ~/.bashrc` (or `source ~/.zshrc`), then `hermes`.
  "The installer handles everything automatically — all dependencies (Python, Node.js, ripgrep, ffmpeg), the repo clone, virtual environment, global `hermes` command setup, and LLM provider configuration."
- **Inputs / options:** Post-install reconfiguration commands, verbatim: `hermes model` "Choose your LLM provider and model"; `hermes tools` "Configure which tools are enabled"; `hermes gateway setup` "Set up messaging platforms"; `hermes config set` "Set individual config values"; `hermes config get` "Inspect individual config values"; `hermes setup` "Or run the full setup wizard to configure everything at once". Installer flags documented on this page: `--skip-browser` (skip the Playwright step) and `--skip-computer-use` (opt out of pre-installing `cua-driver`), both passed as `curl … | bash -s -- --flag`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Clones the repo, creates a venv, symlinks the `hermes` launcher, writes `~/.hermes/`.
- **Config / env:** `HERMES_HOME` (root-mode data directory override).
- **Edge cases / guards:** Troubleshooting table verbatim (Problem | Solution): "`hermes: command not found`" | "Reload your shell (`source ~/.bashrc`) or check PATH"; "`API key not set`" | "Run `hermes model` to configure your provider, or `hermes config set OPENROUTER_API_KEY your_key`"; "Missing config after update" | "Run `hermes config check` then `hermes config migrate`".
- **Rebuild notes:** Ship a single installer script that is idempotent, detects the platform, and manages its own toolchain (uv-pinned Python, downloaded Node) rather than relying on system packages. Better: emit a machine-readable install receipt naming every path it created.

### Install layout: per-user vs root-mode (FHS)  `id: docs-rest.install-layout`
- **Surface:** Docs / Core
- **Where:** Installation → "### What the Installer Does" → **"#### Install Layout"**.
- **What it does:** Documents where code, the `hermes` binary, and data live for the two installer modes.
- **How it works:** Table verbatim (Installer | Code lives at | `hermes` binary | Data directory): "Per-user (git installer)" | `~/.hermes/hermes-agent/` | `~/.local/bin/hermes` (symlink) | `~/.hermes/`. "Root-mode (`sudo curl … \| sudo bash`)" | `/usr/local/lib/hermes-agent/` | `/usr/local/bin/hermes` | `/root/.hermes/` (or `$HERMES_HOME`). Rationale verbatim: "The root-mode **FHS layout** (`/usr/local/lib/…`, `/usr/local/bin/hermes`) matches where other system-wide developer tools land on Linux. It's useful for shared-machine deployments where one system install should serve every user. Per-user config (auth, skills, sessions) still lives under each user's `~/.hermes/` or explicit `HERMES_HOME`."
- **Inputs / options:** Running the installer with or without `sudo`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Directory creation at the paths above.
- **Config / env:** `HERMES_HOME`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** Even in root mode, per-user state stays per user.
- **Rebuild notes:** Split *code location* from *data location* and let a single env var (`HERMES_HOME`) relocate only the latter. Better: support a fully read-only code prefix (`/opt`, Nix store) as a first-class mode.

### Installer prerequisites and bundled toolchain  `id: docs-rest.install-prereqs`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Installation → **"## Prerequisites"**.
- **What it does:** Names the only true prerequisites and lists the four dependencies the installer provides itself.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "On non-Windows platforms, the only prerequisite is **Git**. On Linux, also make sure `curl` and `xz-utils` are available (the installer downloads Node.js as a `.tar.xz` archive). The desktop app additionally requires `g++` (or `build-essential` on Debian/Ubuntu) to compile native modules." Auto-installed: **uv** ("fast Python package manager"), **Python 3.11** ("via uv, no sudo needed"), **Node.js v26** ("for browser automation and WhatsApp bridge; existing system Node 22.22+, 24.11+, or 26+ is used as-is"), **ripgrep** ("fast file search"), **ffmpeg** ("audio format conversion for TTS").
- **Inputs / options:** `sudo apt install curl xz-utils`; `sudo apt install build-essential`; verification `git --version`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Downloads and installs the toolchain into the install prefix.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** Existing system Node in the accepted ranges (22.22+, 24.11+, 26+) is reused rather than replaced.
- **Rebuild notes:** Accept an existing runtime only above an explicit minimum, otherwise vendor your own. Better: record the resolved toolchain versions in the install receipt so `doctor` can compare.

### Non-sudo / system-service-user installs  `id: docs-rest.install-nonsudo`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Installation → **"## Non-Sudo / System Service User Installs"**.
- **What it does:** Documents installing and running Hermes as a dedicated unprivileged user (e.g. a `hermes` systemd account) with no sudo.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "The only thing on the install path that genuinely needs root is Playwright's `--with-deps` step, which `apt`-installs shared libraries (`libnss3`, `libxkbcommon`, etc.) used by Chromium. The installer detects whether sudo is available and gracefully degrades when it isn't — it will install the Chromium binary into the service user's own Playwright cache and print the exact command an administrator needs to run separately."
- **Inputs / options:** Recommended split (Debian/Ubuntu), all four steps verbatim — (1) as admin: `sudo npx playwright install-deps chromium`; (2) as service user: `curl -fsSL https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/install.sh | bash`, or with `bash -s -- --skip-browser`; the installer also pre-installs `cua-driver` unless `--skip-computer-use` is passed; (3) PATH fix — Option A `echo 'export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"' >> ~/.bashrc`, Option B `sudo ln -s /home/hermes/.hermes/hermes-agent/venv/bin/hermes /usr/local/bin/hermes`; (4) verify with `hermes doctor`; (5) gateway persistence `sudo loginctl enable-linger <service-user>`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Installs into the service user's home; may leave Chromium system libs missing until the admin step runs.
- **Config / env:** `PATH` must include `~/.local/bin`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'dotenv'` means "you're invoking the repo source `hermes` file (`~/.hermes/hermes-agent/hermes`) with system Python instead of the venv launcher (`~/.hermes/hermes-agent/venv/bin/hermes`)". Arch uses pacman with the same sudo detection; Fedora/RHEL and openSUSE "don't support `--with-deps` at all, so an administrator always installs the system libraries separately. The relevant `dnf`/`zypper` commands are printed by the installer." User-level services "stop at logout and do not start at boot until you enable lingering".
- **Rebuild notes:** Split the install into a root-only "system libraries" phase and a user-only phase, and detect sudo rather than assuming it. Better: ship the browser dependency check as part of `doctor` with a copy-paste admin command.

### Install method auto-detection  `id: docs-rest.install-method-autodetect`
- **Surface:** Docs / Core
- **Where:** Installation → **"## Install method auto-detection"**.
- **What it does:** Documents that Hermes detects whether it was installed via the git installer, Docker, or NixOS, and that `hermes update` prints the matching update command.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "There's no env var to set — the detection is based on the install layout (`~/.hermes/hermes-agent/` checkout, Docker image stamp, or Nix store path). `hermes doctor` also surfaces the detected method under its environment summary."
- **Inputs / options:** n/a
- **Outputs / side effects:** Changes the update command shown; blocks in-place updates on image/package-managed installs.
- **Config / env:** None (deliberately no env override).
- **Edge cases / guards:** Docker installs are additionally identified by a read-only provenance marker (see `docs-rest.update-image-provenance`).
- **Rebuild notes:** Derive install kind from filesystem facts, never from a mutable env var, and fail closed when the marker is damaged.

### Platform Support tiers (Tier 1 / Tier 2 / Unsupported)  `id: docs-rest.platform-support`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Docs → Getting Started → **"Platform Support"** (`getting-started/platform-support.md`, `sidebar_position: 2.5`).
- **What it does:** Defines the support contract for every OS and distribution method.
- **How it works:** Three sections.
  **"## Tier 1"** — "We strive to never break installations and updates for these. Issues & regressions in Tier 1 are our first priority and take precedence over other platforms." Rows: **macOS (Apple Silicon)** — Hermes Desktop, `install.sh`; **Windows 10 / 11** (x86_64, aarch64) — Hermes Desktop, `install.ps1`, note "A few features are [not available]"; **Linux / WSL2** (x86_64, aarch64) — `install.sh`, note "We test on the latest Ubuntu and WSL2. If your distro has glibc, systemd, and follows the Filesystem Hierarchy Standard, it's likely to work pretty well."; **Docker Container** (x86_64, aarch64) — `docker pull`, note "Docker installs do not support `hermes update`. Updating is done by running a new image."
  **"## Tier 2"** — "These platforms are maintained in-tree only as a best effort. Releases may break them, and we can't promise we'll fix them promptly when they break. PRs will be accepted to fix issues with them, but they will take precedence below fixing issues with Tier 1 platforms." Rows: **Android (Termux)** (aarch64) — `install.sh`, "A few features are [not available]"; **Nix** (MacOS, Linux, NixOS) — `install.sh`, "Breaks often due to node.js packaging woes. Best of luck~! <3".
  **"## Unsupported"** — "They may be broken right now, they may break more in the future. PRs to fix them will _not_ be accepted, and any code that keeps compatibility with them may be removed at any point." Items verbatim: "installs via the AUR (we might upstream patches if it helps out <3)"; "macOS on x86 (Intel) processors"; "installs via `pypi` (e.g. `uv tool install hermes-agent`, `pip install hermes-agent`, etc.)"; "installs via `brew` (`brew install hermes-agent`)".
- **Inputs / options:** n/a
- **Outputs / side effects:** n/a
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** Unsupported installs may be removed from the codebase without notice.
- **Rebuild notes:** Publish an explicit, tiered support matrix keyed on (OS, arch, distribution method) with an escalation policy per tier. Better: gate CI jobs on the tier so Tier-1 breakage cannot merge.

### Learning Path page (level + use-case routing)  `id: docs-rest.learning-path`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Docs → Getting Started → **"Learning Path"** (`getting-started/learning-path.md`, `sidebar_position: 3`).
- **What it does:** Routes readers to a reading order by experience level, by use case, and by feature.
- **How it works:** Three routers.
  **"## By Experience Level"** (Level | Goal | Recommended Reading | Time Estimate): **Beginner** "Get up and running, have basic conversations, use built-in tools" → Installation → Quickstart → CLI Usage → Configuration, "~1 hour"; **Intermediate** "Set up messaging bots, use advanced features like memory, cron jobs, and skills" → Sessions → Messaging → Tools → Skills → Memory → Cron, "~2–3 hours"; **Advanced** "Build custom tools, create skills, train models with RL, contribute to the project" → Architecture → Adding Tools → Creating Skills → Contributing, "~4–6 hours".
  **"## By Use Case"** — seven scenarios, each an ordered reading list: **"I want a CLI coding assistant"** (Installation, Quickstart, CLI Usage, Code Execution, Context Files, Tips & Tricks); **"I want a Telegram/Discord bot"** (Installation, Configuration, Messaging Overview, Telegram Setup, Discord Setup, Voice Mode, Use Voice Mode with Hermes, Security; plus project examples Daily Briefing Bot and Team Telegram Assistant); **"I want to automate tasks"** (Quickstart, Cron Scheduling, Batch Processing, Delegation, Hooks); **"I want a team of specialist Bots"** (Desktop, Profiles, Bot Mode, Cron Scheduling, Multi-connection Desktop); **"I want to build custom tools/skills"** (Plugins, Build a Hermes Plugin, Tools Overview, Skills Overview, MCP, Architecture, Adding Tools, Creating Skills); **"I want to train models"** (Quickstart, Configuration, Atropos RL Environments (external `https://github.com/NousResearch/atropos`), Provider Routing, Architecture); **"I want to use it as a Python library"** (Installation, Quickstart, Python Library Guide, Architecture, Tools, Sessions).
  **"## Key Features at a Glance"** — 12 rows (Feature | What It Does): **Tools** "Built-in tools the agent can call (file I/O, search, shell, etc.)"; **Skills** "Installable plugin packages that add new capabilities"; **Memory** "Persistent memory across sessions"; **Bot Mode** "Named specialist Bots with persistent chats, routines, group chats, and `@mentions`"; **Context Files** "Feed files and directories into conversations"; **MCP** "Connect to external tool servers via Model Context Protocol"; **Cron** "Schedule recurring agent tasks"; **Delegation** "Spawn sub-agents for parallel work"; **Code Execution** "Run Python scripts that call Hermes tools programmatically"; **Browser** "Web browsing and scraping"; **Hooks** "Event-driven callbacks and middleware"; **Batch Processing** "Process multiple inputs in bulk"; **Provider Routing** "Route requests across multiple LLM providers".
  **"## What to Read Next"** — six state-based next steps ("Just finished installing?", "Completed the Quickstart?", "Comfortable with the basics?", "Setting up for a team?", "Ready to build?", "Want practical examples?").
- **Inputs / options:** n/a
- **Outputs / side effects:** n/a
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** Tip warns "For most custom tool creation, start with plugins. The [Adding Tools] page is for built-in Hermes core development, not the usual user/custom-tool path."
- **Rebuild notes:** Provide three orthogonal indexes into the same docs (level, goal, feature) instead of one linear TOC. Better: track which pages a reader has opened and compute the remaining path.

### `hermes update` — the update pipeline (5 steps)  `id: docs-rest.update-pipeline`
- **Surface:** Docs / CLI
- **Where:** Docs → Getting Started → **"Updating & Uninstalling"** (`getting-started/updating.md`, `sidebar_position: 3`) → "## Updating" → "### What happens during an update".
- **What it does:** Documents the five ordered phases every `hermes update` run performs.
- **How it works:** Verbatim, in order:
  1. **"Pre-update snapshot"** — "a lightweight state snapshot is saved by default (covers pairing data, cron jobs, `config.yaml`, `.env`, `auth.json`, and other state files that get modified at runtime; individual files over 1 GiB are skipped so a large sessions DB never slows the update down). Because the code swap and gateway restarts touch every profile, the same snapshot is taken for **every profile** on the install — each into its own `state-snapshots/` directory — and the post-update cron-jobs safety net checks each profile against its own snapshot." Controlled by `updates.pre_update_backup`. "Quick snapshots are file-loss recovery, not code-rollback insurance — for a coherent point-in-time rollback use `--backup` (full mode)."
  2. **"Git pull"** — "pulls the latest code from the `main` branch and updates submodules".
  3. **"Post-pull syntax validation + auto-rollback"** — "after the pull, Hermes compiles the nine critical files every `hermes` invocation imports at startup. If any fails to parse (e.g. an orphan merge-conflict marker, an accidentally truncated file), Hermes runs `git reset --hard <pre-pull-sha>` to roll the install back so your shell stays bootable."
  4. **"Dependency install"** — "runs `uv pip install -e \".[all]\"` to pick up new or changed dependencies".
  5. **"Config migration"** — "detects new config options added since your version and prompts you to set them".
  6. **"Gateway auto-restart"** — "running gateways are refreshed after the update completes so the new code takes effect immediately. Service-managed gateways (systemd on Linux, launchd on macOS) are restarted through the service manager. Manual gateways are relaunched automatically when Hermes can map the running PID back to a profile. Manually-launched `hermes serve` / `hermes dashboard` backends … each backend records its bind address in the install's spawn ledger at startup, so the update stops it before the code swap and relaunches it afterward on the **same host and port**". "Backends owned by a running Desktop app are left to the app's own respawn."
- **Inputs / options:** Full flag list from `hermes_cli/subcommands/update.py:12-115` — `--gateway` ("Gateway mode: use file-based IPC for prompts instead of stdin (used internally by /update)"), `--check`, `--plan`, `--no-backup`, `--backup`, `--yes`/`-y`, `--keep-stash`, `--branch NAME`, `--switch-branch`, `--force`, `--force-venv`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Snapshot directories under each profile's `state-snapshots/`; a receipt in `~/.hermes/logs/update_receipts/`; a mirrored log at `~/.hermes/logs/update.log`; restarted gateways/backends.
- **Config / env:** `updates.pre_update_backup` (`quick` default | `full` | `off`; legacy `true`⇒`full`, `false`⇒`off`), `updates.non_interactive_local_changes`, `updates.parked_branch_strategy`, `updates.auto_switch_parked_branch`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** Auto-rollback on syntax failure ("Re-run `hermes update` once the upstream fix lands"); expected output block shows the emoji progress lines "📥 Pulling latest code...", "📦 Updating dependencies...", "✅ Dependencies updated", "🔍 Checking for new config options...", "✅ Config is up to date", "🔄 Restarting gateways...", "✅ Gateway restarted", "✅ Hermes Agent updated successfully!".
- **Rebuild notes:** An updater must (a) snapshot mutable state per profile before touching code, (b) validate the new tree parses before letting the shell depend on it, (c) roll back automatically on parse failure, (d) restart every supervised process it knows about, and (e) write a receipt. Better: content-address the code tree so rollback is an atomic symlink swap rather than a `git reset`.

### `hermes update --branch NAME` (non-default branch updates)  `id: docs-rest.update-branch`
- **Surface:** CLI / Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### Updating against a non-default branch: `--branch`".
- **What it does:** Updates against a branch other than `origin/main` — for QA channels, feature branches, or release-candidate testing.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "If your local checkout is on a different branch, Hermes auto-stashes any uncommitted work, switches HEAD to the target branch, and then pulls. Branches that don't exist locally are auto-tracked from `origin/<name>` (`git checkout -B <name> origin/<name>`). Branches that don't exist anywhere fail cleanly — your stashed changes are restored before exit so you're never stranded in a weird state. The `main`-only fork-upstream sync logic is automatically skipped on non-`main` branches." Source: `hermes_cli/subcommands/update.py:73-83`.
- **Inputs / options:** `--branch NAME` (metavar `NAME`, default `None`). Examples: `hermes update --branch release-candidate`; `hermes update --check --branch experimental` ("preview behindness only").
- **Outputs / side effects:** Moves HEAD; may create a local tracking branch; may create and restore a stash.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** Nonexistent branches fail cleanly with the stash restored.
- **Rebuild notes:** Auto-track remote branches and always restore the stash on any failure path. Better: refuse to switch when the target branch's HEAD fails the syntax gate.

### Parked-branch handling and `updates.parked_branch_strategy`  `id: docs-rest.update-parked-branch`
- **Surface:** CLI / Config / Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### Checkout parked on a feature branch".
- **What it does:** Defines what `hermes update` does when the source checkout is sitting on a feature branch instead of the update target.
- **How it works:** Clean tree, branch fully merged ("every commit already contained in `origin/main` — `git cherry` reports nothing unmerged"): the update prints **"Checkout was parked on '&lt;branch&gt;' (fully merged) — switched back to main"** and stays on `main`. Clean tree, unmerged commits: "the update still switches to `main` so the update can proceed — this is what non-interactive callers (the desktop update button, gateway `/update`, cron) rely on… Your commits are untouched: `git checkout` never discards committed work, and the update prints a loud notice naming the branch and commit count, plus the `git checkout <branch>` command to pick the work back up later." Dirty tree: "Hermes does **not** touch it. The code update is marked **SKIPPED** with a loud warning naming the branch, how far behind `origin/main` it is, and the exact commands to resolve". Completion line always shows branch and HEAD: **"✓ Update complete! [main @ 30fcf9580]"**. Code: `hermes_cli/update_cmd.py:1374-1446`.
- **Inputs / options:** `updates.parked_branch_strategy: update_in_place` — "The update then merges `origin/main` **into** your branch instead of switching away from it — the checkout never moves, your commits survive, and the running code advances. Fast-forward when possible; on divergence a true merge behind a `pre-update-<stamp>` safety tag, stopping cleanly (nothing changed) on conflict." `hermes update --switch-branch` "overrides back to the switch path for one run". `updates.auto_switch_parked_branch: false` "to disable the auto-switch entirely (the skip warning still fires)" — refusal reason string in code: **"updates.auto_switch_parked_branch is set to false in config.yaml"** (`hermes_cli/update_cmd.py:1446`).
- **Outputs / side effects:** May move HEAD, may create a `pre-update-<stamp>` tag and a merge commit.
- **Config / env:** `updates.parked_branch_strategy` (default `switch`), `updates.auto_switch_parked_branch` (default `true` — `hermes_cli/update_cmd.py:1386`).
- **Edge cases / guards:** Never touches a dirty tree, even with `--switch-branch`; merge conflict stops with nothing changed.
- **Rebuild notes:** Two named strategies (`switch`, `update_in_place`) plus a per-run override, and a hard "never touch dirty tree" invariant. Better: offer a third strategy that rebases the feature branch onto the new main behind the same safety tag.

### `updates.non_interactive_local_changes` (stash vs discard)  `id: docs-rest.update-noninteractive-local-changes`
- **Surface:** Config / Desktop / Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### Local changes on non-interactive updates"; Desktop app path quoted verbatim: **"Settings → Advanced → In-App Update Local Changes"**.
- **What it does:** Decides what happens to auto-stashed local source edits when the update runs with no terminal (desktop "Update" button, gateway-triggered update).
- **How it works:** Verbatim: `stash` (default) — "auto-stash, pull, then auto-restore your changes on top of the updated code. Nothing is lost; if a restore hits conflicts they're preserved in a git stash for manual recovery." `discard` — "auto-stash and drop the stash after the pull, so the update always lands on a clean tree. Use this only on machines where you never intend to keep local edits to the Hermes source. It stash-drops (not `git reset --hard` + `git clean -fd`), so ignored paths like `node_modules`, `venv`, and build outputs are never touched." Registered as a dashboard-editable key at `hermes_cli/web_server.py:1410`.
- **Inputs / options:** YAML: `updates: { non_interactive_local_changes: stash | discard }`. CLI: `--keep-stash`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Creates/drops a git stash entry named `hermes-update-autostash`.
- **Config / env:** `updates.non_interactive_local_changes`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** "**Desktop updates never auto-restore.** The desktop updater invokes `hermes update --keep-stash`: local source edits are still stashed so the update can proceed, but they are **not** re-applied afterward — they stay parked in `git stash` and the update log prints the exact `git stash apply <ref>` command to bring them back." Manual recovery, verbatim: `cd ~/.hermes/hermes-agent`; `git stash list --format='%gd %H %s'`; `git stash apply stash@{0}`.
- **Rebuild notes:** Non-interactive callers need an explicit policy for un-answerable prompts; never silently reapply edits into an updated tree from a GUI. Better: show the parked stash in the desktop UI with a one-click apply.

### `hermes update --check` (preview only)  `id: docs-rest.update-check`
- **Surface:** CLI / Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### Preview-only: `hermes update --check`".
- **What it does:** Reports whether an update is available without pulling.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "it fetches and compares commits against `origin/main`. No files are modified, no gateway is restarted. Useful in scripts and cron jobs that gate on \"is there an update\"." Help text in code: "Check whether an update is available without installing anything" (`hermes_cli/subcommands/update.py:30-35`).
- **Inputs / options:** `--check` (combinable with `--branch NAME`).
- **Outputs / side effects:** A `git fetch`; no working-tree change.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** Refused with exit code 2 on image-managed installs (see `docs-rest.update-image-provenance`).
- **Rebuild notes:** Separate "is there an update" from "apply it" so automation can gate cheaply.

### `hermes update --plan` (fleet preview)  `id: docs-rest.update-plan`
- **Surface:** CLI / Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### Fleet preview: `hermes update --plan`".
- **What it does:** Prints the full update plan for a machine that runs several profiles or services, without changing anything.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: prints "the install kind (git checkout, Docker image, Nix/apt managed), every running Hermes service across all profiles with its supervisor (systemd, launchd, manual) and the code version it is actually serving, and the restart mechanism each one will get. Manually-launched `hermes serve` / `hermes dashboard` backends appear too (from the spawn ledger), with their recorded bind endpoint and a \"stop before code swap, relaunch with recorded launch args\" restart mechanism. On image- or package-managed installs the plan reports that the install is not updatable in place and names the right update command instead. Read-only and safe on a live fleet." Help text in code (`hermes_cli/subcommands/update.py:33-42`) matches.
- **Inputs / options:** `--plan`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Read-only output; "The same inventory is embedded in every real update's receipt (`~/.hermes/logs/update_receipts/`)".
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** Queries each gateway over its local control socket (`gateway.sock` in the profile's data directory, a named pipe on Windows) when available; older gateways are discovered through their state files.
- **Rebuild notes:** Model an install as a fleet of supervised processes and print the plan before mutating any of them. Better: make `--plan` emit JSON for fleet tooling.

### Update receipts and the fleet version check  `id: docs-rest.update-receipts`
- **Surface:** CLI / Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### Update receipts and the fleet version check".
- **What it does:** Every run writes a machine-readable receipt and post-restart the updater verifies each gateway is actually serving the new code.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "Every `hermes update` run writes a machine-readable receipt to `~/.hermes/logs/update_receipts/` (last 20 kept, `latest.json` always points at the most recent): the pre-update fleet plan, each step taken, anything skipped and why, the gateway restart outcome, and the final fleet version matrix. After the restart phase the updater compares each live gateway's running code against the freshly updated checkout and prints a per-profile matrix — a gateway still serving pre-update code is reported loudly with the exact restart command, and the update exits non-zero so automation never treats a mixed-version fleet as healthy."
- **Inputs / options:** n/a (automatic).
- **Outputs / side effects:** `~/.hermes/logs/update_receipts/*.json` + `latest.json`; non-zero exit on a mixed-version fleet; a `refused` receipt on image-managed installs.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** Retention 20 receipts.
- **Rebuild notes:** Emit a signed receipt per update and verify post-condition (running version == on-disk version) before exiting 0.

### `hermes update --backup` / `updates.pre_update_backup`  `id: docs-rest.update-backup`
- **Surface:** CLI / Config / Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### Full pre-update backup: `--backup`".
- **What it does:** Opts into a full pre-pull zip of `HERMES_HOME` (config, auth, sessions, skills, pairing) for high-value profiles.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "`updates.pre_update_backup` is a single knob with three modes: `quick` (default — the lightweight state snapshot described above), `full` (the quick snapshot plus a complete `HERMES_HOME` zip; can add minutes on large homes), and `off` (no pre-update backup at all — `--no-backup` does the same for a single run). Legacy boolean values still work: `true` means `full`, `false` means `off`." Implementation: `hermes_cli/backup.py:2206-2350` (`_pre_update_backup_dir`, `_prune_pre_update_backups`, `create_pre_update_backup`) and `hermes_cli/main.py:5179-5187` (`_resolve_pre_update_backup_mode`, `_run_pre_update_backup`).
- **Inputs / options:** `--backup` ("Force a FULL pre-update backup (quick state snapshot + HERMES_HOME zip) for this run, regardless of updates.pre_update_backup"); `--no-backup` ("Skip ALL pre-update backups for this run"); YAML `updates: { pre_update_backup: full }`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** A zip in the pre-update backup directory; pruning to a retention count.
- **Config / env:** `updates.pre_update_backup`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** Doc's tip: "Update backups protect an in-place update. If you're migrating your whole setup to different hardware, use `hermes backup` + `hermes import` instead".
- **Rebuild notes:** One tri-state knob with per-run overrides in both directions, plus retention pruning. Better: incremental/de-duplicated snapshots so `full` is cheap.

### Windows update guards (`--force`, `--force-venv`, transactional venv)  `id: docs-rest.update-windows-guards`
- **Surface:** CLI / Docs / Platform:Windows
- **Where:** Updating → "### Windows: another `hermes.exe` is running" and "#### Windows venv recreation is transactional".
- **What it does:** Prevents an update from corrupting a Windows install while another process holds the venv's executables or interpreter.
- **How it works:** Guard 1 refuses when another `hermes.exe` holds the venv entry point. Error text verbatim:
  ```
  ✗ Another hermes.exe is running:
      PID 12345  hermes.exe

    Updating now would fail to overwrite ...\venv\Scripts\hermes.exe because
    Windows blocks REPLACE on a running executable.

    Close Hermes Desktop, exit any open `hermes` REPLs, and
    stop the gateway (`hermes gateway stop`) before retrying.
    Override with `hermes update --force` if you've already
    confirmed those processes will not write to the venv.
  ```
  With `--force` the updater "will still retry the `.exe` rename with exponential backoff and, on stubborn locks, schedule the replacement for next reboot via `MoveFileEx(MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT)`". Guard 2 "refuses to touch the venv while any process is running from its Python interpreter (the Desktop app's backend, a gateway, a Python REPL)… This guard is **not** bypassed by `--force`; if you're certain the detected holders are false positives, use the explicit `hermes update --force-venv`."
  Transactional venv recreation, verbatim: "the Windows installer must recreate an existing `venv`, it first moves the old directory to a unique `venv.stale.*` name, then creates and verifies the replacement. The old tree is deleted only after the dependency install completes and the baseline imports pass in the new tree — until then it is the rollback source (recorded in `venv.pending-backup`)." Failure paths: "If the move cannot be completed, the installer stops and leaves the live `venv` untouched. If `uv` fails or reports success without creating the interpreter, any partial replacement is moved to `venv.failed.*` and the previous venv is restored."
- **Inputs / options:** `--force` ("Windows: proceed with the update even when another hermes.exe is detected. The concurrent process will likely cause WinError 32 warnings. Does NOT bypass the venv-process guard"), `--force-venv` ("Windows: mutate the venv even while other processes are running from its interpreter … Use only if you know the detected holders are false positives"), per `hermes_cli/subcommands/update.py:97-113`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** `venv.stale.*` / `venv.failed.*` / `venv.pending-backup` directories; possibly a pending-reboot file replacement.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** Stale/failed directories may persist when another process holds a handle; "Close Hermes Desktop, gateways, and Python processes using the install, then retry the install/update; parked directories are cleaned up best-effort after a successful recreation."
- **Rebuild notes:** On Windows, treat venv replacement as move-then-verify-then-delete, and separate "executable locked" from "interpreter in use" into two guards with two distinct overrides.

### Update survives terminal disconnect (SIGHUP + update.log)  `id: docs-rest.update-sighup`
- **Surface:** CLI / Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### If your terminal disconnects mid-update".
- **What it does:** Makes `hermes update` survive a dropped SSH session or closed terminal window.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "The update ignores `SIGHUP`, so closing your SSH session or terminal window no longer kills it mid-install. `pip` and `git` child processes inherit this protection, so the Python environment cannot be left half-installed by a dropped connection." "All output is mirrored to `~/.hermes/logs/update.log` while the update runs." "`Ctrl-C` (SIGINT) and system shutdown (SIGTERM) are still honored — those are deliberate cancellations, not accidents." "You no longer need to wrap `hermes update` in `screen` or `tmux` to survive a terminal drop."
- **Inputs / options:** `tail -f ~/.hermes/logs/update.log`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** `~/.hermes/logs/update.log`.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** SIGINT/SIGTERM still terminate.
- **Rebuild notes:** Ignore SIGHUP for the whole process group during a mutating operation and mirror stdout to a durable log. Better: make the update resumable from the log's last completed phase.

### Recommended post-update validation  `id: docs-rest.update-post-validation`
- **Surface:** Docs / CLI
- **Where:** Updating → "### Recommended Post-Update Validation".
- **What it does:** A five-step confirmation checklist after an update.
- **How it works:** Verbatim, in order: 1. `git status --short` — "if the tree is unexpectedly dirty, inspect before continuing"; 2. `hermes doctor` — "checks config, dependencies, and service health"; 3. `hermes --version` — "confirm the version bumped as expected"; 4. "If you use the gateway: `hermes gateway status`"; 5. "If `doctor` reports npm audit issues: run `npm audit fix` in the flagged directory". Warning admonition **":::warning Dirty working tree after update"** — "If `git status --short` shows unexpected changes after `hermes update`, stop and inspect them before continuing. This usually means local modifications were reapplied on top of the updated code, or a dependency step refreshed lockfiles."
- **Inputs / options:** The five commands above.
- **Outputs / side effects:** None (read-only checks except `npm audit fix`).
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** n/a
- **Rebuild notes:** Publish a fixed post-update checklist and make `doctor` cover all of it so the list collapses to one command.

### Updating from messaging platforms (`/update`)  `id: docs-rest.update-from-messaging`
- **Surface:** Gateway/Telegram / Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### Updating from Messaging Platforms".
- **What it does:** Lets an authorized user run the update from Telegram, Discord, Slack, WhatsApp, or Teams by sending `/update`.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "This pulls the latest code, updates dependencies, and restarts running gateways. The bot will briefly go offline during the restart (typically 5–15 seconds) and then resume." Internally the gateway path uses `hermes update --gateway` ("use file-based IPC for prompts instead of stdin (used internally by /update)", `hermes_cli/subcommands/update.py:21-26`).
- **Inputs / options:** Slash command `/update`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Same as a CLI update, plus a gateway restart (5–15 s offline).
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** Prompts cannot be answered over chat, so IPC-based prompt answering replaces stdin.
- **Rebuild notes:** For chat-triggered self-update, replace stdin prompting with a file-based IPC channel and announce the expected downtime.

### Manual update and rollback procedures  `id: docs-rest.update-manual-rollback`
- **Surface:** Docs
- **Where:** Updating → "### Manual Update" and "### Rollback instructions".
- **What it does:** Documents the by-hand equivalents for source installs and how to roll back to a commit or release tag.
- **How it works:** Manual update, verbatim: `cd /path/to/hermes-agent`; `export VIRTUAL_ENV="$HOME/.hermes/venvs/hermes-dev"`; `export PATH="$VIRTUAL_ENV/bin:$PATH"`; `git pull origin main`; `uv pip install -e ".[all]"`; `hermes config check`; `hermes config migrate`. Rollback, verbatim: `git log --oneline -10`; `git checkout <commit-hash>`; `uv pip install -e ".[all]"`; `hermes gateway restart`; to a tag `git checkout vX.Y.Z` (example given: "a recent release like `v2026.5.16`, or any earlier tag from `git tag --sort=-version:refname`").
- **Inputs / options:** As listed.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Moves HEAD; reinstalls dependencies; restarts the gateway.
- **Config / env:** `VIRTUAL_ENV`, `PATH`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** **":::warning"** — "Rolling back may cause config incompatibilities if new options were added. Run `hermes config check` after rolling back and remove any unrecognized options from `config.yaml` if you encounter errors."
- **Rebuild notes:** Make the config schema version-aware so a downgrade can quarantine unknown keys instead of erroring.

### Image-managed installs: the provenance marker  `id: docs-rest.update-image-provenance`
- **Surface:** Docs / Core / Platform:Docker
- **Where:** Updating → "### Image-managed installs (Docker): the provenance marker".
- **What it does:** Makes `hermes update` refuse cleanly on Docker/Nix/apt-managed installs and print the correct update command.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "Published Docker images bake a small read-only marker (`/etc/hermes/image-provenance.json`) that authoritatively identifies the filesystem as image-managed. `hermes update`, `hermes update --check`, and the dashboard's Update button all consult it before touching anything: on an image-managed install they refuse cleanly (exit code 2), print the actual update command (`docker pull nousresearch/hermes-agent:latest`), and write a `refused` receipt so fleet tooling can see the attempt happened. The marker wins even when a source checkout is bind-mounted into the container — the refusal is based on what the running filesystem *is*, not what it looks like. A damaged marker still refuses (fail-closed). Nix- and apt-managed installs refuse through the same gate using the existing detection."
- **Inputs / options:** n/a
- **Outputs / side effects:** Exit code 2; a `refused` receipt in `~/.hermes/logs/update_receipts/`.
- **Config / env:** `/etc/hermes/image-provenance.json` (read-only marker).
- **Edge cases / guards:** Fail-closed on a damaged marker; bind-mounted source checkouts do not defeat it.
- **Rebuild notes:** Put an immutable provenance marker in the image and treat it as the sole authority for "can I self-update". Better: sign the marker so a bind mount cannot forge one.

### Note for Nix users on updating  `id: docs-rest.update-nix-note`
- **Surface:** Docs / Platform:Nix
- **Where:** Updating → "### Note for Nix users".
- **What it does:** Points Nix installs at the Nix package manager for updates and rollbacks.
- **How it works:** Verbatim commands: `nix flake update hermes-agent` ("Update the flake input"); `nix profile upgrade hermes-agent` ("Or rebuild with the latest"); `nix profile rollback` ("Nix installations are immutable — rollback is handled by Nix's generation system").
- **Inputs / options:** As listed.
- **Outputs / side effects:** New Nix generation; rollback restores the previous one.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** "Nix is no longer an explicitly supported install path (best-effort only)".
- **Rebuild notes:** Delegate updates to the package manager that owns the files; never self-update an immutable store.

### `hermes uninstall` and manual uninstall  `id: docs-rest.uninstall`
- **Surface:** CLI / Docs
- **Where:** Updating & Uninstalling → **"## Uninstalling"**.
- **What it does:** Removes the Hermes install, optionally keeping `~/.hermes/`.
- **How it works:** Verbatim: "The uninstaller gives you the option to keep your configuration files (`~/.hermes/`) for a future reinstall." Manual: `rm -f ~/.local/bin/hermes`; `rm -rf /path/to/hermes-agent`; `rm -rf ~/.hermes` ("Optional — keep if you plan to reinstall").
- **Inputs / options:** `hermes uninstall` (interactive keep/remove config choice).
- **Outputs / side effects:** Deletes the code tree and launcher; optionally deletes `~/.hermes/`.
- **Config / env:** n/a
- **Edge cases / guards:** **":::info"** — if installed as a system service, stop and disable first: `hermes gateway stop`; Linux `systemctl --user disable hermes-gateway`; macOS `launchctl remove ai.hermes.gateway`. **":::tip Moving to a new machine rather than leaving?"** — "`hermes backup` captures the entire `~/.hermes` directory including credentials, while `hermes profile export` packs a single profile with credentials excluded by design (so an export alone is not a full backup)."
- **Rebuild notes:** Always separate "remove code" from "remove data" and default to keeping data. Better: offer an export-then-uninstall single flow.

### Android / Termux install (Tier 2)  `id: docs-rest.termux`
- **Surface:** Docs / Platform:Android
- **Where:** Docs → Getting Started → **"Android / Termux"** (`getting-started/termux.md`, `sidebar_position: 3`), heading **"Hermes on Android with Termux"**.
- **What it does:** Documents the tested Termux bundle, the one-line installer path, the fully explicit manual path, and Android-specific limitations.
- **How it works:** The tested bundle maps to `python -m pip install -e '.[termux]' -c constraints-termux.txt` and installs: "the Hermes CLI", "cron support", "PTY/background terminal support", "Telegram gateway support (manual / best-effort background runs)", "MCP support", "Honcho memory support", "ACP support".
  **Option 1: One-line installer** — `curl -fsSL https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/install.sh | bash`. On Termux the installer automatically: "uses `pkg` for system packages"; "creates the venv with `python -m venv`"; "attempts the broad `.[termux-all]` extra first and falls back to the smaller `.[termux]` extra (then a base install) — the curl installer matches this order automatically"; "links `hermes` into `$PREFIX/bin` so it stays on your Termux PATH"; "skips the untested browser / WhatsApp bootstrap".
  **Option 2: Manual install (fully explicit)** — six numbered steps: (1) `pkg update` + `pkg install -y git python clang rust make pkg-config libffi openssl nodejs ripgrep ffmpeg` (rationale per package: `python` "runtime + venv support", `git` "clone/update the repo", `clang`, `rust`, `make`, `pkg-config`, `libffi`, `openssl` "needed to build a few Python dependencies on Android", `nodejs` "optional Node runtime for experiments beyond the tested core path", `ripgrep` "fast file search", `ffmpeg` "media / TTS conversions"); (2) `git clone https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent.git && cd hermes-agent`; (3) `python -m venv venv`, `source venv/bin/activate`, `export ANDROID_API_LEVEL="$(getprop ro.build.version.sdk)"`, `python -m pip install --upgrade pip setuptools wheel` ("`ANDROID_API_LEVEL` is important for Rust / maturin-based packages such as `jiter`"); (4) `python -m pip install -e '.[termux]' -c constraints-termux.txt` (minimal alternative `python -m pip install -e '.' -c constraints-termux.txt`); (5) `ln -sf "$PWD/venv/bin/hermes" "$PREFIX/bin/hermes"`; (6) `hermes --version` + `hermes doctor`; (7) `hermes`.
- **Inputs / options:** Follow-up setup: `hermes model`, `hermes setup`. Browser options — cloud providers (Browserbase, Browser Use, Firecrawl) need only `pkg install nodejs-lts` (`agent-browser` "resolves lazily via `npx agent-browser` on first use"); local browser automation needs `pkg install nodejs-lts` + `npm install -g agent-browser && agent-browser install` ("the bare npx fallback is deliberately rejected in local mode as too fragile to advertise as ready"). "The browser tool automatically includes Termux directories (`/data/data/com.termux/files/usr/bin`) in its PATH search".
- **Outputs / side effects:** Venv under the clone; `hermes` symlink in `$PREFIX/bin`.
- **Config / env:** `ANDROID_API_LEVEL`, `PREFIX`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** "What is not part of the tested path yet": "`.[all]` is not supported on Android today"; "the `voice` extra is blocked by `faster-whisper -> ctranslate2`, and `ctranslate2` does not publish Android wheels"; "automatic browser / Playwright bootstrap is skipped in the Termux installer"; "Docker-based terminal isolation is not available inside Termux"; "Android may still suspend Termux background jobs, so gateway persistence is best-effort rather than a normal managed service". Troubleshooting entries: "`No solution found` when installing `.[all]`", "`uv pip install` fails on Android", "`jiter` / `maturin` complains about `ANDROID_API_LEVEL`", "`hermes doctor` says ripgrep or Node is missing" (`pkg install ripgrep nodejs`), "Build failures while installing Python packages" (`pkg install clang rust make pkg-config libffi openssl`). "Known limitations on phones": "Docker backend is unavailable"; "local voice transcription via `faster-whisper` is unavailable in the tested path"; "browser automation setup is intentionally skipped by the installer"; "some optional extras may work, but only `.[termux]` and `.[termux-all]` are currently documented as the tested Android bundles". Bug-report template asks for: "your Android version", "`termux-info`", "`python --version`", "`hermes doctor`", "the exact install command and full error output".
- **Rebuild notes:** Ship a named, constraint-pinned extra per constrained platform (`[termux]`, `[termux-all]`) and have the installer try the broad one then fall back. Better: publish prebuilt wheels for the blocking native deps.

### Community-maintained Termux APT package (`pkg install hermes-agent`)  `id: docs-rest.termux-apt`
- **Surface:** Docs / Platform:Android
- **Where:** Termux page → **"## Community-maintained native `pkg` option"**.
- **What it does:** Documents a third-party APT repository that ships Hermes as a native Termux package.
- **How it works:** Verbatim caution **":::caution Contributor-operated distribution"** — "This APT repository is **community-maintained by `@adybag14-cyber` and is not an official NousResearch distribution**. NousResearch does not build, sign, host, or audit these packages. Enabling the repository means trusting the contributor-operated repository and its signing key." Sources: `adybag14-cyber/termux-python` (repo bootstrap + packaging) and `adybag14-cyber/termux-hermes` (Hermes package build).
- **Inputs / options:** `curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/adybag14-cyber/termux-python/main/scripts/setup_apt_repo.sh | bash`; `pkg install hermes-agent`; updates `pkg update` + `pkg upgrade hermes-agent`. Documented signing-key fingerprint: `EAD24A2124EFA7393A78B7B14699F966313F7A6B`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Adds a third-party APT source and key; installs Hermes as a package.
- **Config / env:** Install method is recorded as `apt`.
- **Edge cases / guards:** "APT-managed Hermes installs are marked with install method `apt`. Hermes therefore does not run its Git self-updater against package-owned files; use the package manager instead." "Packaging/repository/signing problems for this option should be reported to the community packaging repositories above."
- **Rebuild notes:** Recognize package-manager ownership as a first-class install method and disable the self-updater for it.

### Nix flake quick start (`nix run` / `nix profile install`)  `id: docs-rest.nix-quickstart`
- **Surface:** Docs / Platform:Nix
- **Where:** Docs → Getting Started → **"Nix & NixOS Setup"** (`getting-started/nix-setup.md`) → **"## Quick Start (Any Nix User)"**.
- **What it does:** Installs or runs Hermes straight from the flake with no clone.
- **How it works:** Four levels table verbatim (Level | Who it's for | What you get): "**`nix run` / `nix profile install`**" | "Any Nix user (macOS, Linux)" | "Pre-built binary with all deps — then use the standard CLI workflow"; "**Home Manager module**" | "An agent for one person, on any distribution or on macOS" | "Declarative configuration and a user service, without root"; "**NixOS module (native)**" | "NixOS server deployments" | "Declarative config, hardened systemd service, managed secrets"; "**NixOS module (container)**" | "Agents that need self-modification" | "Everything above, plus a persistent Ubuntu container where the agent can `apt`/`pip`/`npm install`". What differs from the standard install, verbatim: "every Python dependency is a Nix derivation built by [uv2nix](https://github.com/pyproject-nix/uv2nix), and runtime tools (Node.js, git, ripgrep, ffmpeg) are wrapped into the binary's PATH. There is no runtime pip, no venv activation, no `npm install`."
- **Inputs / options:** `nix run github:NousResearch/hermes-agent#desktop`; `nix profile install github:NousResearch/hermes-agent#desktop`; `nix run github:NousResearch/hermes-agent -- setup`; `nix run github:NousResearch/hermes-agent -- --tui`; `nix profile install github:NousResearch/hermes-agent` then `hermes setup` / `hermes --tui`. From a local clone: `git clone …`, `cd hermes-agent`, `nix develop`, `hermes setup`. Flake package variants: `default` (all libraries, "~700 MB to the closure"), `#messaging` ("adds just ~33 MB"), `#full`.
- **Outputs / side effects:** Puts `hermes`, `hermes-agent`, and `hermes-acp` on PATH after `nix profile install`.
